My brain goes straight to, “You blew it. You let yourself slip.”\n\nAnd I know one pound isn’t, like, real weight in a practical sense. I know that. But in the moment it doesn’t feel logical. It feels like people are going to notice, like they’re going to look at me and think I’m getting soft or that I don’t have control of myself. Especially at work, where everybody’s watching fitness and performance, even if they’re not actually saying anything.\n\nSo yeah, I worry about it. Probably a lot more than I should.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about how your body looks when you gain weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, I don’t love saying that, because it sounds kind of vain or shallow, but it’s not really like I’m trying to look good for the sake of looking good. It feels tied to whether I feel ready, if that makes sense.\n\nLike before work, when I’m getting dressed or shaving, I’ll catch myself checking in the mirror more than I probably need to. I’ll look at my waist, or my face, and I’m kind of looking to see if everything looks… sharp enough, I guess. Lean enough. If my face looks a little fuller or my waist doesn’t look how I think it should, it can throw me off before I even get to the station.\n\nAnd then I start feeling less confident, like I’m not as squared away or not as command-ready that day. Which I know sounds dramatic, because obviously whether my face looks a certain way in the mirror shouldn’t decide if I can do my job. But in my head, if I look softer, I feel less in control, and then it kind of follows me around.\n\nDoctor: Is your worry about gaining weight mostly about feeling like you're losing control over your body or your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s pretty much it. I mean, the weight itself is part of it, but the bigger thing is what it means in my head.\n\nLike if I eat a bigger meal at the station, or if I’ve had more water than usual, and then the scale is up even one pound, I don’t just think, “Okay, that’s normal fluctuation.” I get this tight, panicky feeling, like I’ve blown it somehow. Like I let my guard down. And then my mind jumps straight to people noticing — like they’re going to see me as soft, or like I don’t have control over myself.\n\nIt sounds kind of extreme when I say it out loud, because logically I know one pound doesn’t mean anything. But in the moment it feels bigger than that. It feels like if I can’t control that number, then I’m not as disciplined as I’m supposed to be. Like I’m slipping. So yeah, it’s definitely about control… over my body, but also kind of over how people see me and whether I feel like I’ve got myself together.\n\nDoctor: Is there a specific weight or body shape that you think would be unacceptable to you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if there’s, like, an exact number where I’d say, “That’s unacceptable.” It’s more visual than that for me, I think.\n\nLike before work, when I’m getting ready, I’ll look in the mirror and I’m checking my waist and my face almost automatically. I’m looking to see if I still look sharp, I guess — like lean enough, squared away enough. And if my waist looks different, or my face looks a little fuller, even if nobody else would notice it, it gets in my head.\n\nIt can mess with my confidence for the whole day. I’ll feel less… command-ready, if that makes sense. Like I don’t have the same authority or discipline behind me. I know that probably sounds like a lot to put on how my face or waist looks in a mirror, but that’s kind of where my mind goes. So it’s not one specific body shape exactly, but anything that looks softer or less sharp than what I expect from myself feels really hard for me to tolerate.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be afraid of gaining weight if it didn't change your body shape?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, probably. Or at least I’d still be afraid if the scale changed, even if I couldn’t see it right away.\n\nBecause for me it’s not only the shape part, though that definitely matters. It’s also that number feeling like proof that I lost control. Like if we’ve had a big station meal, or I drank more water than usual during a shift, and then I step on the scale and it’s up even one pound, my brain doesn’t calmly go, “That’s probably just food or water.” It’s more like this tight panic in my chest, like, “You blew it. You let yourself slip.”\n\nAnd then I start imagining other people noticing somehow, even if my body doesn’t actually look different. Like they’re going to see me as softer, or less disciplined, or like I don’t have control over myself. I know that doesn’t totally make sense if nothing visibly changed, but the number itself kind of becomes the problem. It feels like evidence against me, I guess.\n\nSo yeah… if gaining weight didn’t change my body shape at all, I think I’d still have a hard time with it. Maybe the mirror part would be less intense, but the scale going up would still mess with me.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight more about how you think you'll look to others or about feeling like you're not in control of your eating or body?\n\nPatient: It’s kind of both, honestly. I don’t know how to separate them all the way.\n\nLike, if I eat a bigger meal at the station — something everyone else would probably just not think twice about — or if I’ve been drinking more water because it’s been a rough shift, and then I get on the scale and it’s up even a pound, my first reaction isn’t logical. I don’t think, “Okay, that’s probably just food or water.” I get this tight panic feeling, like right in my chest, like I’ve blown it somehow.\n\nAnd then it turns into this control thing immediately. Like, “You let your guard down. You weren’t disciplined enough. You’re slipping.” But almost at the same time, my mind goes to how other people might see me. Like they’re going to notice something, even if realistically they probably can’t. I start thinking they’ll see me as soft, or out of control, or like I don’t have myself together.\n\nSo I guess the control part is the core of it, maybe. But the way people might see me is wrapped up in that. If I feel like I’m not controlling my body or what I eat, then I assume it’s going to show, and then I feel exposed. Like everyone can tell I failed, even from one pound, which I know sounds ridiculous when I say it out loud.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight mostly about feeling like you're losing control over your body or is it more about feeling like you're losing control in general?\n\nPatient: I think it starts with my body, but it turns into this bigger “I’m losing control” feeling really fast.\n\nLike if I have a bigger station meal, or I drink more water than usual because we’ve been busy, and then later I step on the scale and it’s up even one pound, I don’t react like it’s just normal fluctuation. I know, logically, it could be food or water or whatever, but in the moment it feels like I blew it. Like I let something slip that I’m supposed to be on top of.\n\nAnd then my head kind of runs with it. It’s not just, “Oh, my body changed.” It becomes, “You’re getting soft. You’re not disciplined. People are going to see that you’re out of control.” Even if no one could possibly notice anything, I feel like somehow it’s visible, like it says something about me.\n\nSo… I guess mostly it’s about control over my body, but it connects to control in general. If my weight goes up, even a little, it makes me feel like I’m failing at keeping myself together.\n\nDoctor: Is there a particular part of your body where you're most concerned about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… mostly my waist and my face, I think.\n\nLike before work, when I’m getting ready, I’ll catch myself looking in the mirror way longer than I need to. I’m not just checking if I look presentable. I’m looking at whether my waist looks tight enough, or if my face looks sharp enough — like if my jaw or cheeks look a certain way. And if they don’t, or if I think they look softer that day, it can really throw me off.\n\nIt sounds kind of stupid saying it, but it affects how I feel walking into the station. Like if I look sharp, I feel more put together, more ready, more… I don’t know, command-ready, I guess. Like I can handle myself and people will take me seriously. But if my waist feels bigger or my face looks puffy or not defined enough, then I feel off before the day even starts. Less confident. Like I’m already behind somehow.\n\nSo yeah, those are the main areas — waist and face. Those are the ones I check the most and get stuck on.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's a specific body ideal or image that you're trying to maintain?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t know if it’s like a specific person or some picture I’m trying to copy, exactly. It’s more this image in my head of looking sharp and lean and controlled, if that makes sense.\n\nLike before work, when I’m in the mirror, I’m not just thinking, “Do I look okay?” I’m checking whether my waist looks tight enough, whether my face looks defined enough — like my jaw, my cheeks, just whether I look… sharp. And if I don’t, if my face looks a little softer or my waist feels off, it really messes with me more than I want to admit.\n\nIt affects how I walk into the station. If I feel like I look the way I’m supposed to, I feel more confident, more put together, more ready to be taken seriously. Like I can carry myself better. But if I don’t, then I feel kind of wrong all day, like I’m less command-ready or something. I know that sounds dramatic, because obviously my ability to do the job shouldn’t depend on how my face looks in the mirror that morning, but emotionally it feels connected.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is partly because you believe it would affect your performance as a lieutenant?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it is, at least partly.\n\nIt’s not like I literally think one pound would make me unable to do the job. I know that doesn’t make sense. But if the scale is up even a pound after, like, a bigger station meal or because I drank more water during a busy shift, I still get this tight panic in my chest. Like I’ve blown it somehow.\n\nAnd then it turns into this whole story in my head — that I’m getting soft, or I’m losing control, and people are going to be able to see it. Not even necessarily that they’d say anything, but that they’d look at me and think I’m not as disciplined, not as put together. And as a lieutenant, that hits harder, because I feel like I’m supposed to look and act like I have myself under control.\n\nSo yeah, I think the fear is tied to performance, but maybe more through what gaining weight would mean about me. Like if I can’t control that, then I worry it says I can’t control myself in general, and then I don’t feel as solid walking into work.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight would decrease if you weren't trying to become a lieutenant?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, but… honestly, I don’t think it would just go away.\n\nTrying to become a lieutenant definitely adds pressure, because I feel like people are watching how I carry myself and whether I seem disciplined. But even if you took that away, I think I’d still have that reaction to gaining weight. Like if the scale is up even one pound after a big station meal, or if I drank a lot of water during a busy shift, my brain doesn’t calmly go, “That’s normal.” I get this tight panic, like I’ve already messed something up.\n\nAnd then I start thinking people are going to see it — like they’ll look at me and think I’m soft, or out of control, or not sharp enough. It’s embarrassing because logically I know one pound isn’t something everyone can see, and it doesn’t mean anything about who I am. But in the moment it feels really real.\n\nSo… yeah, the lieutenant thing probably makes it worse. But I think the fear is still there underneath it. It’s more like the job gives it a reason to be louder.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your fear of gaining weight is connected to a broader fear of losing control in your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s actually a big part of it.\n\nIt’s not just the number itself, even though I get very stuck on the number. Like if I step on the scale and it’s up even one pound after a big station meal, or because I drank more water during a shift, I don’t take it like, “Okay, that’s normal.” I get this tight panic in my chest, like I’ve blown it somehow. Like I let something slip.\n\nAnd then my mind goes straight to what that means about me. Like I’m getting soft, or I’m not disciplined enough, or people are going to notice and see me as less in control. It’s not even that anyone has to say anything. I just imagine them seeing it on me somehow, and thinking I’m not as sharp or put together.\n\nSo yeah, I guess the weight thing feels connected to control in a bigger way. If the scale goes up, it doesn’t feel like just weight — it feels like proof that I’m losing control of myself. And that scares me more than I like admitting.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight more about the actual weight or body shape itself, or is it about what that weight or shape represents to you in terms of your identity or how you think others see you?\n\nPatient: I think it’s both, but if I’m being honest, it’s probably more about what it represents.\n\nLike, I do get focused on the actual shape. Before work, I’ll look in the mirror and I’m not just checking if my uniform is straight. I’m looking at my waist, my face, whether I look… sharp enough, I guess. If my face looks fuller to me, or my waist doesn’t look the way I think it should, it can really throw me off before I even get out the door.\n\nAnd it’s not just, “I don’t like how I look.” It turns into, “Do I look ready? Do I look like someone people can trust in charge?” Like if I don’t look lean or sharp, I feel less command-ready, if that makes sense. My confidence drops before I’ve even done anything wrong.\n\nSo the weight or shape matters to me, but I think the bigger thing is what I read into it. I see it as discipline, control, being put together. And if I don’t look that way to myself, I start worrying that other people can see the same thing and that they’ll think I’m not as solid as I’m supposed to be.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like gaining weight would change who you are as a person?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it would literally change who I am, like my values or what I care about, but… it feels like it would change how I see myself, if that makes sense. And once that shifts, it kind of feels like I’m a different version of myself.\n\nLike before work, I’ll catch myself in the mirror longer than I probably need to. I’m supposed to just be checking my uniform, making sure everything’s set, but I end up looking at my waist, my face, whether I look sharp enough. And if I don’t look the way I think I should — if my face looks a little fuller or my waist looks off — it gets in my head immediately.\n\nIt’s not just, “Oh, I don’t like that.” It turns into this whole thing where I feel less confident walking into the station. Like I don’t feel as command-ready, or as put together. And I know gaining weight wouldn’t actually make me less capable at my job or less of a person, but emotionally it feels like it would make me… not myself. Or not the version of myself I’m trying to be.\n\nSo I guess no, it wouldn’t change who I am deep down. But it would change how solid I feel in myself, and that can feel pretty close.\n\nDoctor: Do you think gaining weight would make you feel less masculine?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t love saying that, because it sounds kind of shallow or maybe like I’m buying into some stereotype, but yeah, gaining weight would make me feel less masculine in some way.\n\nFor me it’s tied to looking sharp, I guess. Before work, when I’m in uniform, I’ll check the mirror and I’m not just making sure my shirt is tucked right or my gear looks okay. I’m looking at my waist, my face, whether I look lean enough, whether I look like I have that… solid, controlled look. And if my face looks fuller to me, or my waist looks softer, it really messes with me.\n\nIt’s not just about appearance. It makes me feel less confident walking into the station, like I’m not as command-ready or as put together. And I know logically that masculinity shouldn’t come down to a waistline or how sharp my face looks that morning, but emotionally it kind of does in that moment. I start feeling like I don’t look the part, and then I don’t feel the part either.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I think that’s a big part of it. Not the whole thing maybe, but definitely part of it.

When I’m leaner, I feel more… in control, I guess. More sharp, more solid. And I know that sounds tied up with masculinity, but that’s honestly how it feels in my head. Like if I look lean, I feel like I look disciplined, like I can handle myself, like I fit the image I’m supposed to have.

The scale really gets to me with that. Like if we have a big station meal, or I drink a lot of water, and then the number is up even a pound, I know there are normal reasons for that. I know one pound doesn’t mean anything permanent. But my body reacts before my brain catches up. I get this tight, panicky feeling, like I’ve blown it somehow. Like suddenly I’m slipping, or I’m going to look softer, and people are going to notice.

And it’s not just “I gained a pound.” It turns into, “People are going to see me as soft,” or “They’re going to think I’m out of control.” So yeah… being lean feels connected to being masculine for me, because I associate it with being disciplined and strong and not letting myself go. I don’t like that I think that way, but that’s where my mind goes.

And once that shifts, it kind of feels like I’m a different version of myself.\n\nLike before work, I’ll catch myself in the mirror longer than I probably need to. I’m supposed to just be checking my uniform, making sure everything’s set, but I end up looking at my waist, my face, whether I look sharp enough. And if I don’t look the way I think I should — if my face looks a little fuller or my waist looks off — it gets in my head immediately.\n\nIt’s not just, “Oh, I don’t like that.” It turns into this whole thing where I feel less confident walking into the station. Like I don’t feel as command-ready, or as put together. And I know gaining weight wouldn’t actually make me less capable at my job or less of a person, but emotionally it feels like it would make me… not myself. Or not the version of myself I’m trying to be.\n\nSo I guess no, it wouldn’t change who I am deep down. But it would change how solid I feel in myself, and that can feel pretty close.\n\nDoctor: Do you think gaining weight would make you feel less masculine?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t love saying that, because it sounds kind of shallow or maybe like I’m buying into some stereotype, but yeah, gaining weight would make me feel less masculine in some way.\n\nFor me it’s tied to looking sharp, I guess. Before work, when I’m in uniform, I’ll check the mirror and I’m not just making sure my shirt is tucked right or my gear looks okay. I’m looking at my waist, my face, whether I look lean enough, whether I look like I have that… solid, controlled look. And if my face looks fuller to me, or my waist looks softer, it really messes with me.\n\nIt’s not just about appearance. It makes me feel less confident walking into the station, like I’m not as command-ready or as put together. And I know logically that masculinity shouldn’t come down to a waistline or how sharp my face looks that morning, but emotionally it kind of does in that moment. I start feeling like I don’t look the part, and then I don’t feel the part either.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is partly because you associate being lean with being more masculine?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think that’s a big part of it. Not the whole thing maybe, but definitely part of it.\n\nWhen I’m leaner, I feel more… in control, I guess. More sharp, more solid. And I know that sounds tied up with masculinity, but that’s honestly how it feels in my head. Like if I look lean, I feel like I look disciplined, like I can handle myself, like I fit the image I’m supposed to have.\n\nThe scale really gets to me with that. Like if we have a big station meal, or I drink a lot of water, and then the number is up even a pound, I know there are normal reasons for that. I know one pound doesn’t mean anything permanent. But my body reacts before my brain catches up. I get this tight, panicky feeling, like I’ve blown it somehow. Like suddenly I’m slipping, or I’m going to look softer, and people are going to notice.\n\nAnd it’s not just “I gained a pound.” It turns into, “People are going to see me as soft,” or “They’re going to think I’m out of control.” So yeah… being lean feels connected to being masculine for me, because I associate it with being disciplined and strong and not letting myself go. I don’t like that I think that way, but that’s where my mind goes.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I came in because I think what started as me trying to eat cleaner has kind of gotten away from me. I’m trying to get on the lieutenant track at the department, and at first it was just, you know, cutting out junk, being more disciplined, packing better food for shifts, that kind of thing.\n\nBut lately it’s more like… I’ll get through a whole shift day on black coffee, maybe a protein shake, and then a small dinner later, and I know that doesn’t really add up to enough. Especially with the calls and training and just being on my feet — I know I’m burning way more than that. I keep telling myself it’s just being focused or staying lean, but it’s started to feel like I’m cutting meals down more and more, and I’m having a hard time backing off from it.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I mean, I didn’t really think of it as “significant” at first, because in my head it was just, like, tightening things up and getting more disciplined, but it’s noticeable now.\n\nMy turnout gear and even my regular station clothes are hanging looser on me than they used to. Like, stuff that fit pretty normally before just kind of sits different now. More than I like admitting.\n\nIt’s not even always some huge amount. Like, if we have a big station meal — somebody makes pasta or chili or whatever — or if I’ve had more water than usual and the scale is up even a pound the next morning, I feel this immediate tightness in my chest, like I messed everything up. It feels like people are going to notice, like they’re going to look at me and think I’m getting soft or that I don’t have control of myself. Especially at work, where everybody’s watching fitness and performance, even if they’re not actually saying anything.\n\nSo yeah, I worry about it. Probably a lot more than I should.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about how your body looks when you gain weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, I don’t love saying that, because it sounds kind of vain or shallow, but it’s not really like I’m trying to look good for the sake of looking good. It feels tied to whether I feel ready, if that makes sense.\n\nLike before work, when I’m getting dressed or shaving, I’ll catch myself checking in the mirror more than I probably need to. I’ll look at my waist, or my face, and I’m kind of looking to see if everything looks… sharp enough, I guess. Lean enough. If my face looks a little fuller or my waist doesn’t look how I think it should, it can throw me off before I even get to the station.\n\nAnd then I start feeling less confident, like I’m not as squared away or not as command-ready that day. Which I know sounds dramatic, because obviously whether my face looks a certain way in the mirror shouldn’t decide if I can do my job. But in my head, if I look softer, I feel less in control, and then it kind of follows me around.\n\nDoctor: Is your worry about gaining weight mostly about feeling like you're losing control over your body or your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s pretty much it. I mean, the weight itself is part of it, but the bigger thing is what it means in my head.\n\nLike if I eat a bigger meal at the station, or if I’ve had more water than usual, and then the scale is up even one pound, I don’t just think, “Okay, that’s normal fluctuation.” I get this tight, panicky feeling, like I’ve blown it somehow. Like I let my guard down. I’m looking to see if I still look sharp, I guess — like lean enough, squared away enough. And if my waist looks different, or my face looks a little fuller, even if nobody else would notice it, it gets in my head.\n\nIt can mess with my confidence for the whole day. I’ll feel less… command-ready, if that makes sense. Like I don’t have the same authority or discipline behind me. I know that probably sounds like a lot to put on how my face or waist looks in a mirror, but that’s kind of where my mind goes. So it’s not one specific body shape exactly, but anything that looks softer or less sharp than what I expect from myself feels really hard for me to tolerate.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be afraid of gaining weight if it didn't change your body shape?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, probably. Or at least I’d still be afraid if the scale changed, even if I couldn’t see it right away.\n\nBecause for me it’s not only the shape part, though that definitely matters. It’s also that number feeling like proof that I lost control. You let yourself slip.”\n\nAnd then I start imagining other people noticing somehow, even if my body doesn’t actually look different. Like they’re going to see me as softer, or less disciplined, or like I don’t have control over myself. I know that doesn’t totally make sense if nothing visibly changed, but the number itself kind of becomes the problem. It feels like evidence against me, I guess.\n\nSo yeah… if gaining weight didn’t change my body shape at all, I think I’d still have a hard time with it. Maybe the mirror part would be less intense, but the scale going up would still mess with me.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight more about how you think you'll look to others or about feeling like you're not in control of your eating or body?\n\nPatient: It’s kind of both, honestly. I don’t know how to separate them all the way.\n\nLike, if I eat a bigger meal at the station — something everyone else would probably just not think twice about — or if I’ve been drinking more water because it’s been a rough shift, and then I get on the scale and it’s up even a pound, my first reaction isn’t logical. I don’t think, “Okay, that’s probably just food or water.” I get this tight panic feeling, like right in my chest, like I’ve blown it somehow.\n\nAnd then it turns into this control thing immediately. Like, “You let your guard down. You weren’t disciplined enough. You’re slipping.” But almost at the same time, my mind goes to how other people might see me. Like they’re going to notice something, even if realistically they probably can’t. I start thinking they’ll see me as soft, or out of control, or like I don’t have myself together.\n\nSo I guess the control part is the core of it, maybe. But the way people might see me is wrapped up in that. If I feel like I’m not controlling my body or what I eat, then I assume it’s going to show, and then I feel exposed. I know, logically, it could be food or water or whatever, but in the moment it feels like I blew it. If my weight goes up, even a little, it makes me feel like I’m failing at keeping myself together.\n\nDoctor: Is there a particular part of your body where you're most concerned about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… mostly my waist and my face, I think.\n\nLike before work, when I’m getting ready, I’ll catch myself looking in the mirror way longer than I need to. I’m not just checking if I look presentable. I’m looking at whether my waist looks tight enough, or if my face looks sharp enough — like if my jaw or cheeks look a certain way. I know that doesn’t make sense. But if the scale is up even a pound after, like, a bigger station meal or because I drank more water during a busy shift, I still get this tight panic in my chest. Like I’ve blown it somehow.\n\nAnd then it turns into this whole story in my head — that I’m getting soft, or I’m losing control, and people are going to be able to see it. Not even necessarily that they’d say anything, but that they’d look at me and think I’m not as disciplined, not as put together. And as a lieutenant, that hits harder, because I feel like I’m supposed to look and act like I have myself under control.\n\nSo yeah, I think the fear is tied to performance, but maybe more through what gaining weight would mean about me. Like if I can’t control that, then I worry it says I can’t control myself in general, and then I don’t feel as solid walking into work.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight would decrease if you weren't trying to become a lieutenant?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, but… honestly, I don’t think it would just go away.\n\nTrying to become a lieutenant definitely adds pressure, because I feel like people are watching how I carry myself and whether I seem disciplined. But even if you took that away, I think I’d still have that reaction to gaining weight. Like if the scale is up even one pound after a big station meal, or if I drank a lot of water during a busy shift, my brain doesn’t calmly go, “That’s normal.” I get this tight panic, like I’ve already messed something up.\n\nAnd then I start thinking people are going to see it — like they’ll look at me and think I’m soft, or out of control, or not sharp enough. It’s embarrassing because logically I know one pound isn’t something everyone can see, and it doesn’t mean anything about who I am. But in the moment it feels really real.\n\nSo… yeah, the lieutenant thing probably makes it worse. But I think the fear is still there underneath it. It’s more like the job gives it a reason to be louder.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your fear of gaining weight is connected to a broader fear of losing control in your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s actually a big part of it.\n\nIt’s not just the number itself, even though I get very stuck on the number. Like if I step on the scale and it’s up even one pound after a big station meal, or because I drank more water during a shift, I don’t take it like, “Okay, that’s normal.” I get this tight panic in my chest, like I’ve blown it somehow. I just imagine them seeing it on me somehow, and thinking I’m not as sharp or put together.\n\nSo yeah, I guess the weight thing feels connected to control in a bigger way. If the scale goes up, it doesn’t feel like just weight — it feels like proof that I’m losing control of myself. And that scares me more than I like admitting.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight more about the actual weight or body shape itself, or is it about what that weight or shape represents to you in terms of your identity or how you think others see you?\n\nPatient: I think it’s both, but if I’m being honest, it’s probably more about what it represents.\n\nLike, I do get focused on the actual shape. I see it as discipline, control, being put together. And if I don’t look that way to myself, I start worrying that other people can see the same thing and that they’ll think I’m not as solid as I’m supposed to be.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like gaining weight would change who you are as a person?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it would literally change who I am, like my values or what I care about, but… it feels like it would change how I see myself, if that makes sense. And once that shifts, it kind of feels like I’m a different version of myself.\n\nLike before work, I’ll catch myself in the mirror longer than I probably need to. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think it’s partly that, yeah, but I don’t know if I’d say it’s mostly masculine in a simple way. It’s more like… staying lean makes me feel like I look sharp and put together, and that overlaps with feeling masculine for me.

Like before work, when I’m getting ready, I’ll look in the mirror and I’m supposed to just be checking my uniform, but I end up really studying my waist and my face. I’m checking whether my face looks defined enough, whether my waist looks tight enough, whether I look like I’m ready to walk into the station and be taken seriously. And if something looks off to me — even if I can’t explain exactly what changed — it can throw off my whole confidence.

It’s like I don’t feel as command-ready that day. I know that sounds dramatic, because nothing about my actual ability changes, but in my head, if I don’t look lean or sharp enough, I feel less solid in myself. So yeah, masculinity is part of it, but it’s also this bigger feeling of needing to look controlled and capable.

If my weight goes up, even a little, it makes me feel like I’m failing at keeping myself together.\n\nDoctor: Is there a particular part of your body where you're most concerned about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… mostly my waist and my face, I think.\n\nLike before work, when I’m getting ready, I’ll catch myself looking in the mirror way longer than I need to. It’s more like… staying lean makes me feel like I look sharp and put together, and that overlaps with feeling masculine for me.\n\nLike before work, when I’m getting ready, I’ll look in the mirror and I’m supposed to just be checking my uniform, but I end up really studying my waist and my face. I’m checking whether my face looks defined enough, whether my waist looks tight enough, whether I look like I’m ready to walk into the station and be taken seriously. And if something looks off to me — even if I can’t explain exactly what changed — it can throw off my whole confidence.\n\nIt’s like I don’t feel as command-ready that day. I know that sounds dramatic, because nothing about my actual ability changes, but in my head, if I don’t look lean or sharp enough, I feel less solid in myself. So yeah, masculinity is part of it, but it’s also this bigger feeling of needing to look controlled and capable.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I came in because I think what started as me trying to eat cleaner has kind of gotten away from me. I’m trying to get on the lieutenant track at the department, and at first it was just, you know, cutting out junk, being more disciplined, packing better food for shifts, that kind of thing.\n\nBut lately it’s more like… I’ll get through a whole shift day on black coffee, maybe a protein shake, and then a small dinner later, and I know that doesn’t really add up to enough. Especially with the calls and training and just being on my feet — I know I’m burning way more than that. I keep telling myself it’s just being focused or staying lean, but it’s started to feel like I’m cutting meals down more and more, and I’m having a hard time backing off from it.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I mean, I didn’t really think of it as “significant” at first, because in my head it was just, like, tightening things up and getting more disciplined, but it’s noticeable now.\n\nMy turnout gear and even my regular station clothes are hanging looser on me than they used to. More than I like admitting.\n\nIt’s not even always some huge amount. I mean, the weight itself is part of it, but the bigger thing is what it means in my head.\n\nLike if I eat a bigger meal at the station, or if I’ve had more water than usual, and then the scale is up even one pound, I don’t just think, “Okay, that’s normal fluctuation.” I get this tight, panicky feeling, like I’ve blown it somehow. Like I let my guard down. And then my mind jumps straight to people noticing — like they’re going to see me as soft, or like I don’t have control over myself.\n\nIt sounds kind of extreme when I say it out loud, because logically I know one pound doesn’t mean anything. But in the moment it feels bigger than that. It feels like if I can’t control that number, then I’m not as disciplined as I’m supposed to be. Like I’m slipping. So yeah, it’s definitely about control… over my body, but also kind of over how people see me and whether I feel like I’ve got myself together.\n\nDoctor: Is there a specific weight or body shape that you think would be unacceptable to you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if there’s, like, an exact number where I’d say, “That’s unacceptable.” It’s more visual than that for me, I think.\n\nLike before work, when I’m getting ready, I’ll look in the mirror and I’m checking my waist and my face almost automatically. I’m looking to see if I still look sharp, I guess — like lean enough, squared away enough. And if my waist looks different, or my face looks a little fuller, even if nobody else would notice it, it gets in my head.\n\nIt can mess with my confidence for the whole day. I’ll feel less… command-ready, if that makes sense. Like I don’t have the same authority or discipline behind me. I know that probably sounds like a lot to put on how my face or waist looks in a mirror, but that’s kind of where my mind goes. So it’s not one specific body shape exactly, but anything that looks softer or less sharp than what I expect from myself feels really hard for me to tolerate.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be afraid of gaining weight if it didn't change your body shape?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, probably. Or at least I’d still be afraid if the scale changed, even if I couldn’t see it right away.\n\nBecause for me it’s not only the shape part, though that definitely matters. It’s also that number feeling like proof that I lost control. Like if we’ve had a big station meal, or I drank more water than usual during a shift, and then I step on the scale and it’s up even one pound, my brain doesn’t calmly go, “That’s probably just food or water.” It’s more like this tight panic in my chest, like, “You blew it. You let yourself slip.”\n\nAnd then I start imagining other people noticing somehow, even if my body doesn’t actually look different. Like they’re going to see me as softer, or less disciplined, or like I don’t have control over myself. It feels like evidence against me, I guess.\n\nSo yeah… if gaining weight didn’t change my body shape at all, I think I’d still have a hard time with it. Maybe the mirror part would be less intense, but the scale going up would still mess with me.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight more about how you think you'll look to others or about feeling like you're not in control of your eating or body?\n\nPatient: It’s kind of both, honestly. I don’t know how to separate them all the way.\n\nLike, if I eat a bigger meal at the station — something everyone else would probably just not think twice about — or if I’ve been drinking more water because it’s been a rough shift, and then I get on the scale and it’s up even a pound, my first reaction isn’t logical. I don’t think, “Okay, that’s probably just food or water.” I get this tight panic feeling, like right in my chest, like I’ve blown it somehow.\n\nAnd then it turns into this control thing immediately. Like, “You let your guard down. You weren’t disciplined enough. You’re slipping.” But almost at the same time, my mind goes to how other people might see me. Like they’re going to notice something, even if realistically they probably can’t. I start thinking they’ll see me as soft, or out of control, or like I don’t have myself together.\n\nSo I guess the control part is the core of it, maybe. But the way people might see me is wrapped up in that. If I feel like I’m not controlling my body or what I eat, then I assume it’s going to show, and then I feel exposed. Like everyone can tell I failed, even from one pound, which I know sounds ridiculous when I say it out loud.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight mostly about feeling like you're losing control over your body or is it more about feeling like you're losing control in general?\n\nPatient: I think it starts with my body, but it turns into this bigger “I’m losing control” feeling really fast.\n\nLike if I have a bigger station meal, or I drink more water than usual because we’ve been busy, and then later I step on the scale and it’s up even one pound, I don’t react like it’s just normal fluctuation. I know, logically, it could be food or water or whatever, but in the moment it feels like I blew it. Like I let something slip that I’m supposed to be on top of.\n\nAnd then my head kind of runs with it. It’s not just, “Oh, my body changed.” It becomes, “You’re getting soft. You’re not disciplined. People are going to see that you’re out of control.” Even if no one could possibly notice anything, I feel like somehow it’s visible, like it says something about me.\n\nSo… I guess mostly it’s about control over my body, but it connects to control in general. If my weight goes up, even a little, it makes me feel like I’m failing at keeping myself together.\n\nDoctor: Is there a particular part of your body where you're most concerned about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… mostly my waist and my face, I think.\n\nLike before work, when I’m getting ready, I’ll catch myself looking in the mirror way longer than I need to. I’m not just checking if I look presentable. I’m looking at whether my waist looks tight enough, or if my face looks sharp enough — like if my jaw or cheeks look a certain way. And if they don’t, or if I think they look softer that day, it can really throw me off.\n\nIt sounds kind of stupid saying it, but it affects how I feel walking into the station. Like if I look sharp, I feel more put together, more ready, more… I don’t know, command-ready, I guess. Like I can handle myself and people will take me seriously. Like I’ve blown it somehow.\n\nAnd then it turns into this whole story in my head — that I’m getting soft, or I’m losing control, and people are going to be able to see it. And as a lieutenant, that hits harder, because I feel like I’m supposed to look and act like I have myself under control.\n\nSo yeah, I think the fear is tied to performance, but maybe more through what gaining weight would mean about me. Like if I can’t control that, then I worry it says I can’t control myself in general, and then I don’t feel as solid walking into work.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight would decrease if you weren't trying to become a lieutenant?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, but… honestly, I don’t think it would just go away.\n\nTrying to become a lieutenant definitely adds pressure, because I feel like people are watching how I carry myself and whether I seem disciplined. But even if you took that away, I think I’d still have that reaction to gaining weight. Like if the scale is up even one pound after a big station meal, or if I drank a lot of water during a busy shift, my brain doesn’t calmly go, “That’s normal.” I get this tight panic, like I’ve already messed something up.\n\nAnd then I start thinking people are going to see it — like they’ll look at me and think I’m soft, or out of control, or not sharp enough. It’s embarrassing because logically I know one pound isn’t something everyone can see, and it doesn’t mean anything about who I am. But in the moment it feels really real.\n\nSo… yeah, the lieutenant thing probably makes it worse. But I think the fear is still there underneath it. It’s more like the job gives it a reason to be louder.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your fear of gaining weight is connected to a broader fear of losing control in your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s actually a big part of it.\n\nIt’s not just the number itself, even though I get very stuck on the number. Like if I step on the scale and it’s up even one pound after a big station meal, or because I drank more water during a shift, I don’t take it like, “Okay, that’s normal.” I get this tight panic in my chest, like I’ve blown it somehow. Like I let something slip.\n\nAnd then my mind goes straight to what that means about me. I just imagine them seeing it on me somehow, and thinking I’m not as sharp or put together.\n\nSo yeah, I guess the weight thing feels connected to control in a bigger way. If the scale goes up, it doesn’t feel like just weight — it feels like proof that I’m losing control of myself. Before work, I’ll look in the mirror and I’m not just checking if my uniform is straight. I’m looking at my waist, my face, whether I look… sharp enough, I guess. If my face looks fuller to me, or my waist doesn’t look the way I think it should, it can really throw me off before I even get out the door.\n\nAnd it’s not just, “I don’t like how I look.” It turns into, “Do I look ready? Do I look like someone people can trust in charge?” Like if I don’t look lean or sharp, I feel less command-ready, if that makes sense. My confidence drops before I’ve even done anything wrong.\n\nSo the weight or shape matters to me, but I think the bigger thing is what I read into it. I see it as discipline, control, being put together. And if I don’t look that way to myself, I start worrying that other people can see the same thing and that they’ll think I’m not as solid as I’m supposed to be.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like gaining weight would change who you are as a person?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it would literally change who I am, like my values or what I care about, but… it feels like it would change how I see myself, if that makes sense. And once that shifts, it kind of feels like I’m a different version of myself.\n\nLike before work, I’ll catch myself in the mirror longer than I probably need to. I’m supposed to just be checking my uniform, making sure everything’s set, but I end up looking at my waist, my face, whether I look sharp enough. And if I don’t look the way I think I should — if my face looks a little fuller or my waist looks off — it gets in my head immediately.\n\nIt’s not just, “Oh, I don’t like that.” It turns into this whole thing where I feel less confident walking into the station. Like I don’t feel as command-ready, or as put together. And I know gaining weight wouldn’t actually make me less capable at my job or less of a person, but emotionally it feels like it would make me… not myself. Or not the version of myself I’m trying to be.\n\nSo I guess no, it wouldn’t change who I am deep down. I don’t love saying that, because it sounds kind of shallow or maybe like I’m buying into some stereotype, but yeah, gaining weight would make me feel less masculine in some way.\n\nFor me it’s tied to looking sharp, I guess. Before work, when I’m in uniform, I’ll check the mirror and I’m not just making sure my shirt is tucked right or my gear looks okay. I’m looking at my waist, my face, whether I look lean enough, whether I look like I have that… solid, controlled look. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think it’s both, but if I’m being honest, the control part is wrapped up in the body shape part so tightly that I don’t always know how to separate them.

Like, the body shape is what I notice first. If I feel like my waist looks different, or my face looks fuller, that’s the thing I can point to. But the panic underneath it feels more like, “I’m losing control.” That’s what happens with the scale especially. If I’ve had a big meal at the station, or even just drank more water, and then the number is up by even a pound, I know there are normal reasons for that. I know that rationally. But it still hits me like I’ve done something wrong.

It’s this tight feeling in my chest and stomach, like I’ve blown it somehow. Like one pound means I’m slipping, even though part of me knows that sounds ridiculous. And then my mind jumps ahead to, “People are going to notice,” or “They’re going to think I’m soft,” or “They’re going to see me as out of control.”

So I guess I’d say the fear starts with wanting to keep a certain shape, but what really scares me is what I think that shape says about me. If I gain, it doesn’t just feel like my body changed. It feels like I failed to keep myself together.

Especially with the calls and training and just being on my feet — I know I’m burning way more than that. I keep telling myself it’s just being focused or staying lean, but it’s started to feel like I’m cutting meals down more and more, and I’m having a hard time backing off from it.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I mean, I didn’t really think of it as “significant” at first, because in my head it was just, like, tightening things up and getting more disciplined, but it’s noticeable now.\n\nMy turnout gear and even my regular station clothes are hanging looser on me than they used to. I mean, the weight itself is part of it, but the bigger thing is what it means in my head.\n\nLike if I eat a bigger meal at the station, or if I’ve had more water than usual, and then the scale is up even one pound, I don’t just think, “Okay, that’s normal fluctuation.” I get this tight, panicky feeling, like I’ve blown it somehow. Like I let my guard down. So yeah, it’s definitely about control… over my body, but also kind of over how people see me and whether I feel like I’ve got myself together.\n\nDoctor: Is there a specific weight or body shape that you think would be unacceptable to you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if there’s, like, an exact number where I’d say, “That’s unacceptable.” It’s more visual than that for me, I think.\n\nLike before work, when I’m getting ready, I’ll look in the mirror and I’m checking my waist and my face almost automatically. I’m looking to see if I still look sharp, I guess — like lean enough, squared away enough. I’ll feel less… command-ready, if that makes sense. Like I don’t have the same authority or discipline behind me. I know that probably sounds like a lot to put on how my face or waist looks in a mirror, but that’s kind of where my mind goes. So it’s not one specific body shape exactly, but anything that looks softer or less sharp than what I expect from myself feels really hard for me to tolerate.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be afraid of gaining weight if it didn't change your body shape?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, probably. Or at least I’d still be afraid if the scale changed, even if I couldn’t see it right away.\n\nBecause for me it’s not only the shape part, though that definitely matters. You let yourself slip.”\n\nAnd then I start imagining other people noticing somehow, even if my body doesn’t actually look different. Like they’re going to see me as softer, or less disciplined, or like I don’t have control over myself. I don’t think, “Okay, that’s probably just food or water.” I get this tight panic feeling, like right in my chest, like I’ve blown it somehow.\n\nAnd then it turns into this control thing immediately. I know, logically, it could be food or water or whatever, but in the moment it feels like I blew it. Like I let something slip that I’m supposed to be on top of.\n\nAnd then my head kind of runs with it. It’s not just, “Oh, my body changed.” It becomes, “You’re getting soft. You’re not disciplined. If my weight goes up, even a little, it makes me feel like I’m failing at keeping myself together.\n\nDoctor: Is there a particular part of your body where you're most concerned about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… mostly my waist and my face, I think.\n\nLike before work, when I’m getting ready, I’ll catch myself looking in the mirror way longer than I need to. And if something looks off to me — even if I can’t explain exactly what changed — it can throw off my whole confidence.\n\nIt’s like I don’t feel as command-ready that day. I know that sounds dramatic, because nothing about my actual ability changes, but in my head, if I don’t look lean or sharp enough, I feel less solid in myself. So yeah, masculinity is part of it, but it’s also this bigger feeling of needing to look controlled and capable.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight driven more by the desire to maintain a certain body shape or by the fear of losing control over your life?\n\nPatient: I think it’s both, but if I’m being honest, the control part is wrapped up in the body shape part so tightly that I don’t always know how to separate them.\n\nLike, the body shape is what I notice first. If I feel like my waist looks different, or my face looks fuller, that’s the thing I can point to. If I’ve had a big meal at the station, or even just drank more water, and then the number is up by even a pound, I know there are normal reasons for that. I know that rationally. And then my mind jumps ahead to, “People are going to notice,” or “They’re going to think I’m soft,” or “They’re going to see me as out of control.”\n\nSo I guess I’d say the fear starts with wanting to keep a certain shape, but what really scares me is what I think that shape says about me. It feels like I failed to keep myself together.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
